## Reducing and recapturing emissions

Further information: [Climate change mitigation](2119179.md)



Global greenhouse gas emission scenarios, based on policies and pledges as of November 2021

Climate change can be mitigated by reducing the rate at which greenhouse gases are emitted into the atmosphere, and by increasing the rate at which carbon dioxide is removed from the atmosphere.[^cite_note-280] To limit global warming to less than 2 °C global greenhouse gas emissions need to be [net-zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_neutrality) by 2070.[^cite_note-IPCC-2018_p12-281] This requires far-reaching, systemic changes on an unprecedented scale in energy, land, cities, transport, buildings, and industry.[^cite_note-282]&#x20;

The [United Nations Environment Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Environment_Programme) estimates that countries need to triple their [pledges under the Paris Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationally_determined_contribution) within the next decade to limit global warming to 2 °C.[^cite_note-283] With pledges made under the Paris Agreement as of 2024, there would be a 66% chance that global warming is kept under 2.8 °C by the end of the century (range: 1.9–3.7 °C, depending on exact implementation and technological progress). When only considering current policies, this raises to 3.1 °C.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_Nations_Environment_Programme202433,_34-284] Globally, limiting warming to 2 °C may result in higher economic benefits than economic costs.[^cite_note-285]&#x20;

Although there is no single pathway to limit global warming to 2 °C,[^cite_note-286] most scenarios and strategies see a major increase in the use of renewable energy in combination with increased energy efficiency measures to generate the needed greenhouse gas reductions.[^cite_note-Teske,_ed._2019_xxiii-287] To reduce pressures on ecosystems and enhance their carbon sequestration capabilities, changes would also be necessary in agriculture and forestry,[^cite_note-288] such as preventing deforestation and restoring natural ecosystems by [reforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reforestation).[^cite_note-289]&#x20;

Other approaches to mitigating climate change have a higher level of risk. Scenarios that limit global warming to 1.5 °C typically project the large-scale use of [carbon dioxide removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide_removal) methods over the 21st century.[^cite_note-290] There are concerns, though, about over-reliance on these technologies, and environmental impacts.[^cite_note-291]&#x20;

[Solar radiation modification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_radiation_modification) (SRM) is a proposal for reducing global warming by reflecting some sunlight away from Earth and back into space. Because it does not reduce greenhouse gas concentrations, it would not address ocean acidification[^cite_note-292] and is not considered mitigation.[^cite_note-293] SRM should be considered only as a supplement to mitigation, not a replacement for it,[^cite_note-294] due to risks such as rapid warming if it were abruptly stopped and not restarted.[^cite_note-295] The most-studied approach is [stratospheric aerosol injection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratospheric_aerosol_injection).[^cite_note-auto-296] SRM could reduce global warming and some of its impacts, though imperfectly.[^cite_note-297] It poses environmental risks, such as changes to rainfall patterns,[^cite_note-298] as well as political challenges, such as who would decide whether to use it.[^cite_note-auto-296]&#x20;

### Clean energy

Main articles: [Sustainable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_energy) and [Sustainable transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_transport)



Coal, oil, and natural gas remain the primary [global energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_energy_consumption) sources even as [renewables](25784.md) have begun rapidly increasing.

[^cite_note-299]

[^cite_note-GlobalCarbonBudget_2025-300]



Wind and solar power, Germany

Renewable energy is key to limiting climate change.[^cite_note-United_Nations_Environment_Programme_2019_46-301] For decades, fossil fuels have accounted for roughly 80% of the world's energy use.[^cite_note-302] The remaining share has been split between nuclear power and renewables (including [hydropower](14073.md), [bioenergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioenergy), wind and solar power and [geothermal energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_energy)).[^cite_note-303] Fossil fuel use is expected to peak in absolute terms prior to 2030 and then to decline, with coal use experiencing the sharpest reductions.[^cite_note-304] Renewables represented 86% of all new electricity generation installed in 2023.[^cite_note-IRENA-305] Other forms of clean energy, such as nuclear and hydropower, currently have a larger share of the energy supply. However, their future growth forecasts appear limited in comparison.[^cite_note-306]&#x20;

While [solar panels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photovoltaic_system) and onshore wind are now among the cheapest forms of adding new power generation capacity in many locations,[^cite_note-307] green energy policies are needed to achieve a rapid transition from fossil fuels to renewables.[^cite_note-308] To achieve carbon neutrality by 2050, renewable energy would become the dominant form of electricity generation, rising to 85% or more by 2050 in some scenarios. Investment in coal would be eliminated and coal use nearly phased out by 2050.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]&#x20;

Electricity generated from renewable sources would also need to become the main energy source for heating and transport.[^cite_note-311] Transport can switch away from [internal combustion engine](41228673.md) vehicles and towards [electric vehicles](279350.md), public transit, and [active transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_mobility) (cycling and walking).[^cite_note-IPCC-2018_p142-312][^cite_note-313] For shipping and flying, low-carbon fuels would reduce emissions.[^cite_note-IPCC-2018_p142-312] Heating could be increasingly decarbonized with technologies like [heat pumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_pump).[^cite_note-314]&#x20;

There are obstacles to the continued rapid growth of clean energy, including renewables.[^cite_note-315] Wind and solar produce energy [intermittently and with seasonal variability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variable_renewable_energy). Traditionally, [hydro dams with reservoirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pumped-storage_hydroelectricity) and fossil fuel power plants have been used when variable energy production is low. Going forward, [battery storage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battery_storage_power_station) can be expanded, [energy demand and supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demand_response) can be matched, and long-distance [transmission](38824.md) can smooth variability of renewable outputs.[^cite_note-United_Nations_Environment_Programme_2019_46-301] Bioenergy is often not carbon-neutral and may have negative consequences for food security.[^cite_note-316] The growth of nuclear power is constrained by controversy around [radioactive waste](37257.md), [nuclear weapon proliferation](22158.md), and [accidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_accident).[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] Hydropower growth is limited by the fact that the best sites have been developed, and new projects are confronting increased social and environmental concerns.[^cite_note-319]&#x20;

[Low-carbon energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life-cycle_greenhouse_gas_emissions_of_energy_sources) improves human health by minimizing climate change as well as reducing air pollution deaths,[^cite_note-320] which were estimated at 7 million annually in 2016.[^cite_note-321] Meeting the Paris Agreement goals that limit warming to a 2 °C increase could save about a million of those lives per year by 2050, whereas limiting global warming to 1.5 °C could save millions and simultaneously increase [energy security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_security) and reduce poverty.[^cite_note-322] Improving air quality also has economic benefits which may be larger than mitigation costs.[^cite_note-323]&#x20;

### Energy conservation

Main articles: [Efficient energy use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_energy_use) and [Energy conservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_conservation)

Reducing energy demand is another major aspect of reducing emissions.[^cite_note-324] If less energy is needed, there is more flexibility for clean energy development. It also makes it easier to manage the electricity grid, and minimizes [carbon-intensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_intensity) infrastructure development.[^cite_note-325] Major increases in energy efficiency investment will be required to achieve climate goals, comparable to the level of investment in renewable energy.[^cite_note-326] Several [COVID-19](62750956.md) related changes in energy use patterns, energy efficiency investments, and funding have made forecasts for this decade more difficult and uncertain.[^cite_note-327]&#x20;

Strategies to reduce energy demand vary by sector. In the transport sector, passengers and freight can switch to more efficient travel modes, such as buses and trains, or use electric vehicles.[^cite_note-328] Industrial strategies to reduce energy demand include improving heating systems and motors, designing less energy-intensive products, and increasing product lifetimes.[^cite_note-329] In the building sector the focus is on better design of new buildings, and higher levels of energy efficiency in retrofitting.[^cite_note-330] The use of technologies like heat pumps can also increase building energy efficiency.[^cite_note-331]&#x20;

### Agriculture and industry

See also: [Sustainable agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_agriculture) and [Green industrial policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_industrial_policy)



Taking into account direct and indirect emissions, industry is the sector with the highest share of global emissions. Data as of 2019 from the IPCC.

&#x20;Agriculture and forestry face a triple challenge of limiting greenhouse gas emissions, preventing the further conversion of forests to agricultural land, and meeting increases in world food demand.[^cite_note-332] A set of actions could reduce agriculture and forestry-based emissions by two-thirds from 2010 levels. These include reducing growth in demand for food and other agricultural products, increasing land productivity, protecting and restoring forests, and reducing greenhouse gas emissions from agricultural production.[^cite_note-333]&#x20;

On the demand side, a key component of reducing emissions is shifting people towards [plant-based diets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant-based_diets).[^cite_note-334] Eliminating the production of livestock for [meat and dairy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_meat_production) would eliminate about three-quarters of all emissions from agriculture and other land use.[^cite_note-335] Livestock also occupy 37% of ice-free land area on Earth and consume feed from the 12% of land area used for crops, driving deforestation and land degradation.[^cite_note-336]&#x20;

Steel and cement production are responsible for about 13% of industrial CO<sub>2</sub> emissions. In these industries, carbon-intensive materials such as coke and lime play an integral role in the production, so that reducing CO<sub>2</sub> emissions requires research into alternative chemistries.[^cite_note-337] Where energy production or CO<sub>2</sub>-intensive [heavy industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_industry) continue to produce waste CO<sub>2</sub>, technology can sometimes be used to capture and store most of the gas instead of releasing it to the atmosphere.[^cite_note-\:22-338] This technology, [carbon capture and storage](3679268.md) (CCS), could have a critical but limited role in reducing emissions.[^cite_note-\:22-338] It is relatively expensive[^cite_note-339] and has been deployed only to an extent that removes around 0.1% of annual greenhouse gas emissions.[^cite_note-\:22-338]&#x20;

### Carbon dioxide removal

Main articles: [Carbon dioxide removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide_removal) and [Carbon sequestration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_sequestration)



Most CO

2

&#x20;emissions have been absorbed by [carbon sinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_sink), including plant growth, soil uptake, and ocean uptake ([2020 Global Carbon Budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Carbon_Project#Global_Carbon_Budget)).

Natural carbon sinks can be enhanced to sequester significantly larger amounts of CO<sub>2</sub> beyond naturally occurring levels.[^cite_note-340] Reforestation and [afforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afforestation) (planting forests where there were none before) are among the most mature sequestration techniques, although the latter raises food security concerns.[^cite_note-341] Farmers can promote sequestration of [carbon in soils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_farming) through practices such as use of winter [cover crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cover_crops), reducing the intensity and frequency of [tillage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tillage), and using compost and manure as soil amendments.[^cite_note-342] Forest and landscape restoration yields many benefits for the climate, including greenhouse gas emissions sequestration and reduction.[^cite_note-Duchelle-2022-133] Restoration/recreation of coastal wetlands, [prairie plots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie_restoration) and [seagrass meadows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seagrass_meadow) increases the uptake of carbon into organic matter.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344] When carbon is sequestered in soils and in organic matter such as trees, there is a risk of the carbon being re-released into the atmosphere later through changes in land use, fire, or other changes in ecosystems.[^cite_note-345]&#x20;

The use of bioenergy in conjunction with carbon capture and storage ([BECCS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioenergy_with_carbon_capture_and_storage)) can result in net negative emissions as CO<sub>2</sub> is drawn from the atmosphere.[^cite_note-346] It remains highly uncertain whether carbon dioxide removal techniques will be able to play a large role in limiting warming to 1.5 °C. Policy decisions that rely on carbon dioxide removal increase the risk of global warming rising beyond international goals.[^cite_note-347]&#x20;

## Adaptation

Main article: [Climate change adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation)

Adaptation is "the process of adjustment to current or expected changes in climate and its effects".[^cite_note-IPCC-2022-348]<sup>: 5 </sup> Without additional mitigation, adaptation cannot avert the risk of "severe, widespread and irreversible" impacts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIPCC_AR5_SYR201417-349] More severe climate change requires more transformative adaptation, which can be prohibitively expensive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIPCC_SR15_Ch42018396–397-350] The [capacity and potential for humans to adapt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptive_capacity) is unevenly distributed across different regions and populations, and developing countries generally have less.[^cite_note-351] The first two decades of the 21st century saw an increase in adaptive capacity in most low- and middle-income countries with improved access to basic [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation) and electricity, but progress is slow. Many countries have implemented adaptation policies. However, there is a considerable gap between necessary and available finance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUNEP2018xii–xiii-352]&#x20;

Adaptation to sea level rise consists of avoiding at-risk areas, learning to live with increased flooding, and building [flood controls](20913655.md). If that fails, [managed retreat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Managed_retreat) may be needed.[^cite_note-353] There are economic barriers for tackling dangerous heat impact. Avoiding strenuous work or having [air conditioning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_conditioning) is not possible for everybody.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthews2018402-354] In agriculture, adaptation options include a switch to more sustainable diets, diversification, erosion control, and genetic improvements for increased tolerance to a changing climate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIPCC_SRCCL_Ch52019439-355] Insurance allows for risk-sharing, but is often difficult to get for people on lower incomes.[^cite_note-356] Education, migration and [early warning systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_warning_system) can reduce climate vulnerability.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIPCC_SR15_Ch42018336–337-357] Planting mangroves or encouraging other coastal vegetation can buffer storms.[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359]&#x20;

Ecosystems adapt to climate change, a process that can be supported by human intervention. By increasing connectivity between ecosystems, species can migrate to more favourable climate conditions. Species can also be [introduced to areas acquiring a favourable climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_migration). Protection and restoration of natural and semi-natural areas helps build resilience, making it easier for ecosystems to adapt. Many of the actions that promote adaptation in ecosystems, also help humans adapt via [ecosystem-based adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem-based_adaptation). For instance, restoration of [natural fire regimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_regime) makes catastrophic fires less likely, and reduces human exposure. Giving rivers more space allows for more water storage in the natural system, reducing flood risk. Restored forest acts as a carbon sink, but planting trees in unsuitable regions can exacerbate climate impacts.[^cite_note-360]&#x20;

There are [synergies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synergy) but also trade-offs between adaptation and mitigation.[^cite_note-361] An example for synergy is increased food productivity, which has large benefits for both adaptation and mitigation.[^cite_note-362] An example of a trade-off is that increased use of air conditioning [allows people to better cope with heat, but increases energy demand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_conditioning_paradox). Another trade-off example is that more compact [urban development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_planning) may reduce emissions from transport and construction, but may also increase the [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) effect, exposing people to heat-related health risks.[^cite_note-363]&#x20;

| Mangrove planting and other habitat conservation can reduce coastal flooding. [Mangrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove) planting and other [habitat conservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitat_conservation) can reduce [coastal flooding](31546082.md). ## Policies and politics

See also: [Politics of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_climate_change) and [Climate change mitigation § Policies](2119179.md#Policies)

[Map](5042951.md)

The [Climate Change Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Change_Performance_Index) ranks countries by greenhouse gas emissions (40% of score), renewable energy (20%), energy use (20%), and climate policy (20%).&#x20;

&#x20; High

&#x20; Medium

&#x20; Low

&#x20; Very low

Countries that are most [vulnerable to climate change](66204363.md) have typically been responsible for a small share of global emissions. This raises questions about justice and fairness.[^cite_note-364] Limiting global warming makes it much easier to achieve the UN's [Sustainable Development Goals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goals), such as eradicating poverty and reducing inequalities. The connection is recognized in [Sustainable Development Goal 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_13) which is to "take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts".[^cite_note-365] The goals on food, clean water and ecosystem protection have synergies with climate mitigation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIPCC_SR15_Ch52018477-366]&#x20;

The [geopolitics](163225.md) of climate change is complex. It has often been framed as a [free-rider problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-rider_problem), in which all countries benefit from mitigation done by other countries, but individual countries would lose from switching to a [low-carbon economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-carbon_economy) themselves. Sometimes mitigation also has localized benefits though. For instance, the benefits of a [coal phase-out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_phase_out) to public health and local environments exceed the costs in almost all regions.[^cite_note-Rauner_2020-367] Furthermore, net importers of fossil fuels win economically from switching to clean energy, causing net exporters to face [stranded assets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stranded_assets): fossil fuels they cannot sell.[^cite_note-368]&#x20;

### Policy options

Further information: [Climate policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_policy)

A wide range of [policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Policy), regulations, and laws are being used to reduce emissions. As of 2019, [carbon pricing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_pricing) covers about 20% of global greenhouse gas emissions.[^cite_note-369] Carbon can be priced with [carbon taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_tax) and [emissions trading systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_emission_trading).[^cite_note-370] Direct global [fossil fuel subsidies](44334234.md) reached \$319 billion in 2017, and \$5.2 trillion when indirect costs such as air pollution are priced in.[^cite_note-371] Ending these can cause a 28% reduction in global carbon emissions and a 46% reduction in air pollution deaths.[^cite_note-372] Money saved on fossil subsidies could be used to support the [transition to clean energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_transition) instead.[^cite_note-373] More direct methods to reduce greenhouse gases include vehicle efficiency standards, renewable fuel standards, and air pollution regulations on heavy industry.[^cite_note-374] Several countries [require utilities to increase the share of renewables in power production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_portfolio_standard).[^cite_note-375] An [Open Coalition on Compliance Carbon Markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Coalition_on_Compliance_Carbon_Markets) with the aim of creating a global cap and trade system was established at COP30 (2025). According to some calculations it can increase emissions reduction seven-fold over current policies, deliver \$200 billion per year for clean-energy and social programs and even close the gap between current emissions trajectory and the goals of the Paris agreement.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378]&#x20;

#### Climate justice

Policy designed through the lens of [climate justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_justice) tries to address human rights issues and social inequality. According to proponents of climate justice, the costs of climate adaptation should be paid by those most responsible for climate change, while the beneficiaries of payments should be those suffering impacts. One way this can be addressed in practice is to have wealthy nations pay poorer countries to adapt.[^cite_note-379]&#x20;

Oxfam found that in 2023 the wealthiest 10% of people were responsible for 50% of global emissions, while the bottom 50% were responsible for just 8%.[^cite_note-380] Production of emissions is another way to look at responsibility: under that approach, the top 21 fossil fuel companies would owe cumulative [climate reparations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_reparations) of \$5.4 trillion over the period 2025–2050.[^cite_note-OneEarth_20230519-381] To achieve a [just transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Just_transition), people working in the fossil fuel sector would also need other jobs, and their communities would need investments.[^cite_note-382]&#x20;

### International climate agreements

Further information: [United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change](31898.md)



Since 2000, rising CO

2

&#x20;emissions in China and the rest of world have surpassed the output of the United States and Europe.

[^cite_note-Friedlingstein_2019-383]

[^cite_note-GlobalCarbonBudgetByRegion_2025-384]



Per person, the United States generates CO

2

&#x20;at a far faster rate than other primary regions.

[^cite_note-Friedlingstein_2019-383]

[^cite_note-GlobalCarbonBudgetPerCapita_2025-385]

Nearly all countries in the world are parties to the 1994 [United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change](31898.md) (UNFCCC).[^cite_note-386] The goal of the UNFCCC is to prevent dangerous human interference with the climate system.[^cite_note-387] As stated in the convention, this requires that greenhouse gas concentrations are stabilized in the atmosphere at a level where ecosystems can adapt naturally to climate change, food production is not threatened, and [economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_analysis_of_climate_change) can be sustained.[^cite_note-388] The UNFCCC does not itself restrict emissions but rather provides a framework for protocols that do. Global emissions have risen since the UNFCCC was signed.[^cite_note-EPA-2019-389] [Its yearly conferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Climate_Change_conference) are the stage of global negotiations.[^cite_note-390]&#x20;

The 1997 [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol) extended the UNFCCC and included legally binding commitments for most developed countries to limit their emissions.[^cite_note-391] During the negotiations, the [G77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_of_77) (representing developing countries) pushed for a mandate requiring [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) to "\[take] the lead" in reducing their emissions,[^cite_note-392] since developed countries contributed most to the [accumulation of greenhouse gases](9528025.md#Cumulative_and_historical_emissions) in the atmosphere. Per-capita emissions were also still relatively low in developing countries and developing countries would need to emit more to meet their development needs.[^cite_note-393]&#x20;

The 2009 [Copenhagen Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copenhagen_Accord) has been widely portrayed as disappointing because of its low goals, and was rejected by poorer nations including the G77.[^cite_note-394] Associated parties aimed to limit the global temperature rise to below 2 °C.[^cite_note-395] The accord set the goal of sending \$100 billion per year to developing countries for mitigation and adaptation by 2020, and proposed the founding of the [Green Climate Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Climate_Fund).[^cite_note-396] As of 2020, only \$83.3 billion were delivered. Only in 2023 the target is expected to be achieved.[^cite_note-397]&#x20;

In 2015 all UN countries negotiated the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md), which aims to keep global warming well below 2.0 °C and contains an aspirational goal of keeping warming under 1.5 °C.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParis_Agreement2015-398] The agreement replaced the Kyoto Protocol. Unlike Kyoto, no binding emission targets were set in the Paris Agreement. Instead, a set of procedures was made binding. Countries have to regularly set ever more ambitious goals and reevaluate these goals every five years.[^cite_note-399] The Paris Agreement restated that developing countries must be financially supported.[^cite_note-400] As of August 2026, 194 states and the European Union have acceded to or [ratified](429650.md) the agreement.[^cite_note-401]&#x20;

The 1987 [Montreal Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal_Protocol), an international agreement to phase out production of ozone-depleting gases, has had benefits for climate change mitigation.[^cite_note-402] Several ozone-depleting gases like [chlorofluorocarbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorofluorocarbons) are powerful greenhouse gases, so banning their production and usage may have avoided a temperature rise of 0.5 °C–1.0 °C,[^cite_note-403] as well as additional warming by preventing damage to vegetation from [ultraviolet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraviolet) radiation.[^cite_note-404] It is estimated that the agreement has been more effective at curbing greenhouse gas emissions than the Kyoto Protocol specifically designed to do so.[^cite_note-405] The most recent amendment to the Montreal Protocol, the 2016 [Kigali Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kigali_Amendment), committed to reducing the emissions of [hydrofluorocarbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrofluorocarbon), which served as a replacement for banned ozone-depleting gases and are also potent greenhouse gases.[^cite_note-406] Should countries comply with the amendment, a warming of 0.3 °C–0.5 °C is estimated to be avoided.[^cite_note-407]&#x20;

### National responses



Annual [CO<sub>2</sub> emissions by region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_carbon_dioxide_emissions). This measures fossil fuel and industry emissions. [Land use change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_use_change) is not included.

[^cite_note-408]

In 2019, the [United Kingdom parliament](13964.md) became the first national government to declare a climate emergency.[^cite_note-409] Other countries and [jurisdictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisdiction) followed suit.[^cite_note-410] That same year, the [European Parliament](9581.md) declared a "climate and environmental emergency".[^cite_note-411] The [European Commission](9974.md) presented its [European Green Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Deal) with the goal of making the EU carbon-neutral by 2050.[^cite_note-412] In 2021, the European Commission released its "[Fit for 55](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fit_for_55)" legislation package, which contains guidelines for the [car industry](163778.md); all new cars on the European market must be [zero-emission vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-emissions_vehicle) from 2035.[^cite_note-413]&#x20;

Major countries in Asia have made similar pledges: South Korea and Japan have committed to become carbon-neutral by 2050, and China by 2060.[^cite_note-414] While India has strong incentives for renewables, it also plans a significant expansion of coal in the country.[^cite_note-415] Vietnam is among very few coal-dependent, fast-developing countries that pledged to phase out unabated coal power by the 2040s or as soon as possible thereafter.[^cite_note-416]&#x20;

As of 2021, based on information from 48 [national climate plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationally_Determined_Contributions) representing 40% of the parties to the Paris Agreement, the estimated total greenhouse gas emissions were 0.5% lower than 2010 levels. This is nowhere near the 45% reduction goal to limit global warming to 1.5 °C or the 25% reduction goal to limit it to 2 °C.[^cite_note-417]&#x20;

## Society and culture

### Denial and misinformation

Further information: [Climate change denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_denial) and [Fossil fuels lobby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuels_lobby)



Data has been [cherry picked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherry_picking) from short periods to falsely assert that global temperatures are not rising. Blue trendlines show short periods that mask longer-term warming trends (red trendlines). Blue rectangle with blue dots shows the so-called [global warming hiatus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming_hiatus).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStover2014-418]

Public debate about climate change has been strongly affected by climate change denial and [misinformation](2203174.md), which first emerged in the United States and has since spread to other countries, particularly Canada and Australia. It originated from fossil fuel companies, industry groups, conservative think tanks, and [contrarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contrarian) scientists.[^cite_note-419] [Like the tobacco industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_industry_playbook), the main strategy of these groups has been to manufacture doubt about climate-change related scientific data and results.[^cite_note-420] People who hold unwarranted doubt about climate change are sometimes called climate change "skeptics", although "contrarians" or "deniers" are more appropriate terms.[^cite_note-421]&#x20;

There are different variants of climate denial: some deny that warming takes place at all, some acknowledge warming but attribute it to natural influences, and some minimize the negative impacts of climate change.[^cite_note-Björnberg_2017-422] Manufacturing uncertainty about the science later developed into a [manufactured controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufactured_controversy): creating the belief that there is significant uncertainty about climate change within the scientific community to delay policy changes.[^cite_note-423] Strategies to promote these ideas include criticism of scientific institutions,[^cite_note-424] and questioning the motives of individual scientists.[^cite_note-Björnberg_2017-422] An [echo chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echo_chamber_\(media\)) of climate-denying [blogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blogs) and media has further fomented misunderstanding of climate change.[^cite_note-425]&#x20;

### Public awareness and opinion

Further information: [Climate communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_communication), [Media coverage of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_climate_change), and [Public opinion on climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion_on_climate_change)



&#x20;The public substantially underestimates the degree of scientific consensus that humans are causing climate change (2022 data).

[^cite_note-KingsCollegeReport_20220629-426]

&#x20;Studies from 2019 to 2021

[^cite_note-Powell2019-427]

[^cite_note-Lynas_2021-5]

[^cite_note-myers_et_al_2021-428]

&#x20;found scientific consensus to range from 98.7 to 100%.

Climate change came to international public attention in the late 1980s.[^cite_note-Weart-429] Due to media coverage in the early 1990s, people often confused climate change with other environmental issues like ozone depletion.[^cite_note-Newell2006-430] [In popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_popular_culture), the [climate fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_fiction) movie *[The Day After Tomorrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Day_After_Tomorrow)* (2004) and the [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) documentary *[An Inconvenient Truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_Inconvenient_Truth)* (2006) focused on climate change.[^cite_note-Weart-429]&#x20;

Significant regional, gender, age and political differences exist in both public concern for, and understanding of, climate change. More highly educated people, and in some countries, women and younger people, were more likely to see climate change as a serious threat.[^cite_note-431] College biology textbooks from the 2010s featured less content on climate change compared to those from the preceding decade, with decreasing emphasis on solutions.[^cite_note-\:0-432] Partisan gaps also exist in many countries,[^cite_note-433] and countries with high [CO<sub>2</sub> emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CO2_emissions) tend to be less concerned.[^cite_note-434] Views on causes of climate change vary widely between countries.[^cite_note-435] Media coverage linked to protests has had impacts on public sentiment as well as on which aspects of climate change are focused upon.[^cite_note-436] Higher levels of worry are associated with stronger public support for policies that address climate change.[^cite_note-437] Concern has increased over time,[^cite_note-438] and in 2021 a majority of citizens in 30 countries expressed a high level of worry about climate change, or view it as a global emergency.[^cite_note-439] A 2024 survey across 125 countries found that 89% of the global population demanded intensified political action, but systematically [underestimated other peoples'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralistic_ignorance) willingness to act.[^cite_note-Damian-25][^cite_note-Peter-26]&#x20;

#### Climate movement

Main articles: [Climate movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_movement) and [Climate change litigation](58562797.md)

Climate protests demand that political leaders take action to prevent climate change. They can take the form of public demonstrations, [fossil fuel divestment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_divestment), lawsuits and other activities.[^cite_note-440][^cite_note-441] Prominent demonstrations include the [School Strike for Climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_Strike_for_Climate). In this initiative, young people across the globe have been protesting since 2018 by skipping school on Fridays, inspired by Swedish activist and then-teenager [Greta Thunberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greta_Thunberg).[^cite_note-442] Mass [civil disobedience](37073.md) actions by groups like [Extinction Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinction_Rebellion) have protested by disrupting roads and public transport.[^cite_note-443]&#x20;

[Litigation](58562797.md) is increasingly used as a tool to strengthen [climate action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_action) from public institutions and companies. Activists also initiate lawsuits which target governments and demand that they take ambitious action or enforce existing laws on climate change.[^cite_note-444] Lawsuits against fossil-fuel companies generally seek compensation for [loss and damage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loss_and_damage_\(climate_change\)).[^cite_note-445] In 2025, the UN's [International Court of Justice](14918.md) issued its advisory opinion, saying explicitly that states must act to stop climate change, and if they fail to accomplish that duty, other states can sue them. This obligation includes implementing their commitments in international agreements they are parties to, such as the 2015 Paris Climate Accord.[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-447][^cite_note-448]&#x20;

## History

For broader coverage of this topic, see [History of climate change science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_climate_change_science).

### Early discoveries



[Eunice Newton Foote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eunice_Newton_Foote) showed carbon dioxide's [heat-capturing effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_effect) in 1856, foreseeing its implications for the planet.

[^cite_note-Foote1856-449]

&#x20;(Carbon dioxide was called "carbonic acid gas".)

Scientists in the 19th century such as [Alexander von Humboldt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_von_Humboldt) began to foresee the effects of climate change.[^cite_note-Nord_2020_p._51-450][^cite_note-Mukherjee_Scanlon_Aureli_Langan_2020_p._331-451][^cite_note-von_Humboldt_Wulf_2018_p._10-452][^cite_note-Erdkamp_Manning_Verboven_2021_p._6-453] In the 1820s, [Joseph Fourier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Fourier) proposed the greenhouse effect to explain why Earth's temperature was higher than the Sun's energy alone could explain. Earth's atmosphere is transparent to sunlight, so sunlight reaches the surface where it is converted to heat. However, the atmosphere is not transparent to heat radiating from the surface, and captures some of that heat, which in turn warms the planet.[^cite_note-454] In 1856 [Eunice Newton Foote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eunice_Newton_Foote) demonstrated that the warming effect of the Sun is greater for air with water vapour than for dry air, and that the effect is even greater with carbon dioxide (CO<sub>2</sub>). In "Circumstances Affecting the Heat of the Sun's Rays" she concluded that "\[a]n atmosphere of that gas would give to our earth a high temperature".[^cite_note-\:2-455][^cite_note-456]&#x20;



This 1912 article succinctly describes the greenhouse effect, how burning coal creates carbon dioxide to cause global warming and climate change.

[^cite_note-Otamatea_Times-457]

Starting in 1859,[^cite_note-458] [John Tyndall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Tyndall) established that nitrogen and oxygen—together totalling 99% of dry air—are transparent to radiated heat. However, water vapour and gases such as methane and carbon dioxide absorb radiated heat and re-radiate that heat into the atmosphere. Tyndall proposed that changes in the concentrations of these gases may have caused climatic changes in the past, including [ice ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_age).[^cite_note-459]&#x20;

[Svante Arrhenius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svante_Arrhenius) noted that water vapour in air continuously varied, but the CO<sub>2</sub> concentration in air was influenced by long-term geological processes. Warming from increased CO<sub>2</sub> levels would increase the amount of water vapour, amplifying warming in a positive feedback loop. In 1896, he published the first climate model of its kind, projecting that halving CO<sub>2</sub> levels could have produced a drop in temperature initiating an ice age. Arrhenius calculated the temperature increase expected from doubling CO<sub>2</sub> to be around 5–6 °C.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELapenis1998-460] Other scientists were initially sceptical and believed that the greenhouse effect was saturated so that adding more CO<sub>2</sub> would make no difference, and that the climate would be self-regulating.[^cite_note-Weart_The_Carbon_Dioxide_Greenhouse_Effect-461] Beginning in 1938, [Guy Stewart Callendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_Stewart_Callendar) published evidence that climate was warming and CO<sub>2</sub> levels were rising,[^cite_note-462] but his calculations met the same objections.[^cite_note-Weart_The_Carbon_Dioxide_Greenhouse_Effect-461]&#x20;

### Development of a scientific consensus

See also: [Scientific consensus on climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_consensus_on_climate_change)



*Scientific consensus on causation:* Academic studies of scientific agreement on human-caused global warming among climate experts (2010–2015) reflect that the level of consensus correlates with expertise in climate science.

[^cite_note-463]

&#x20;A 2019 study found scientific consensus to be at 100%,

[^cite_note-Powell2019-427]

&#x20;and a 2021 study concluded that consensus exceeded 99%.

[^cite_note-Lynas_2021-5]

&#x20;Another 2021 study found that 98.7% of climate experts indicated that the Earth is getting warmer mostly because of human activity.

[^cite_note-myers_et_al_2021-428]

In the 1950s, [Gilbert Plass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilbert_Plass) created a detailed computer model that included different atmospheric layers and the infrared spectrum. This model predicted that increasing CO<sub>2</sub> levels would cause warming. Around the same time, [Hans Suess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Suess) found evidence that CO<sub>2</sub> levels had been rising, and [Roger Revelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Revelle) showed that the oceans would not absorb the increase. The two scientists subsequently helped [Charles Keeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_David_Keeling) to begin a record of continued increase—the "[Keeling Curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keeling_Curve)"[^cite_note-Weart_The_Carbon_Dioxide_Greenhouse_Effect-461]—which was part of continued scientific investigation through the 1960s into possible human causation of global warming.[^cite_note-464] Studies such as the [National Research Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academies_of_Sciences,_Engineering,_and_Medicine)'s 1979 [Charney Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charney_Report) supported the accuracy of climate models that forecast significant warming.[^cite_note-Charney1979-465] Human causation of observed global warming and dangers of unmitigated warming were publicly presented in James Hansen's 1988 testimony before a [US Senate](24909346.md) committee.[^cite_note-nyt19880624-466][^cite_note-history.aip.org2-40] The [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change](15030.md) (IPCC), set up in 1988 to provide formal advice to the world's governments, spurred [interdisciplinary research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interdisciplinarity).[^cite_note-467] As part of the [IPCC reports](15030.md#Assessment_reports), scientists assess the scientific discussion that takes place in [peer-reviewed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review) [journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_journal) articles.[^cite_note-468]&#x20;

There is a nearly unanimous scientific consensus that the climate is warming and that this is caused by human activities.[^cite_note-Lynas_2021-5] No scientific body of national or international standing [disagrees with this view](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_consensus_on_climate_change#Opposing).[^cite_note-469] As of 2019, agreement in recent literature reached over 99%.[^cite_note-Powell2019-427][^cite_note-Lynas_2021-5] The 2021 IPCC Assessment Report stated that it is "unequivocal" that climate change is caused by humans.[^cite_note-Lynas_2021-5] Consensus has further developed that action should be taken to protect people against the impacts of climate change. National science academies have called on world leaders to cut global emissions.[^cite_note-470]&#x20;

### Recent developments

[Extreme event attribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_event_attribution) (EEA), also known as attribution science, was developed in the early decades of the 21st century.[^cite_note-AMS_20161201-471] EEA uses climate models to identify and quantify the role that human-caused climate change plays in the frequency, intensity, duration, and impacts of specific individual extreme weather events.[^cite_note-472][^cite_note-ClimateCentral_20241118-473] Results of attribution studies allow scientists and journalists to make statements such as, "this weather event was made at least *n* times more likely by human-caused climate change" or "this heatwave was made *m* degrees hotter than it would have been in a world without global warming" or "this event was effectively impossible without climate change".[^cite_note-WWA_2021-474]&#x20;

Greater computing power in the 2000s and conceptual breakthroughs in the early to mid 2010s[^cite_note-ScientificAmerican_20170102-475] enabled attribution science to detect the effects of climate change on some events with high confidence.[^cite_note-AMS_20161201-471] Scientists use attribution methods and climate simulations that have already been peer reviewed, allowing "rapid attribution studies" to be published within a "[news cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24-hour_news_cycle)" time frame after weather events.[^cite_note-ScientificAmerican_20170102-475]&#x20;

[^cite_note-37]: &#x20;[IPCC AR5 SYR Glossary 2014](#CITEREFIPCC_AR5_SYR_Glossary2014), p. 120: "Climate change refers to a change in the state of the climate that can be identified (e.g., by using statistical tests) by changes in the mean and/or the variability of its properties and that persists for an extended period, typically decades or longer.
